- REQ-839 WHEN `space` is pressed on a row of the tree, THE tree SHALL mark it, with `●` at the left of the row, or unmark it, and the cursor SHALL stay on the row; WHILE rows are marked, `@` SHALL write the marked rows' paths in the order they were marked, each as REQ-837 writes one and separated by a space, and no other, then let the marks go; a mark SHALL also go when the workspace changes or the tree is closed.
- REQ-840 WHILE a search is on in the tree (REQ-317), `@` and `space` SHALL act on the hit the keys are on exactly as on a row of the tree, and `↵`, `esc` and the typing SHALL do what REQ-317 says; a note row (a folder being read, one that could not be read, the entries not shown) SHALL not be marked or written: `@` and `space` on it do nothing.
- REQ-841 THE keys of the tree that exist (`j`, `k`, `h`, `l`, `↵`, `.`, `/`, `y`, `r`, `t`, `esc`, the click and the wheel: REQ-315 to REQ-318, REQ-615) SHALL work as they did, and the `@` of the field (REQ-408) SHALL work as it did; what is written is text in the field, editable and sent only when the person sends it.
- REQ-842 WHILE the field of REQ-837 has the keyboard, THE leader (`^␣`) SHALL give it back to the client as for any panel, and `t` SHALL then give the keys to the tree when it is shown (and close it only when it has them), so no key is added to go back to the tree; THE list of keys (`^/`) and the help of the tree SHALL name `@` and `space`.
- REQ-843 THE phone SHALL not change (it has no file tree: REQ-408 and the phone's picker, REQ-631, are as they were), and no document of the contract SHALL change: this part reaches the terminal client only.
- REQ-515 WHEN the folder of the name is a worktree already open or exists on disk, or the branch exists, THE box SHALL say which and make nothing; THE sheet SHALL say it for the folder, and the daemon SHALL refuse a branch that exists.
- REQ-83 THE phone SHALL keep its device secret in Keystore-backed storage and SHALL pair from the QR link's text (from the QR the camera reads or from pasted text).
- REQ-84 THE bridge SHALL answer a `mobile_*` command from a phone with an `unsupported` error and not pass it on; THE daemon SHALL end the tunnel it started when it is stopped, and the next start SHALL end one a dead daemon left (recorded in `<state>/tunnel.pid`).
- REQ-85 WHEN a phone pairs, it SHALL show the six-digit code it sent and wait for the PC; WHEN the bridge denies it or the PC cannot be reached, it SHALL say which of expired, declined, locked out, no longer paired or unreachable happened; WHEN the PC revokes it, the phone SHALL forget the pairing and say so.
- REQ-18 IF a client's outbox is full, THEN the daemon SHALL disconnect that client and no other.
- REQ-846 WHEN a conversation is opened to resume a session (REQ-754: from its saved file or by its id) and its transcript ends with an `AskUserQuestion` or `ExitPlanMode` `tool_use` that has no `tool_result` and nothing after it, THE daemon SHALL set the `ask` of a question or a plan as REQ-435 and REQ-436 do, with `restored` true, the tool `ask`, the status `asking` and the attention `question` or `plan`; WHILE the session is held by another `claude` (REQ-575) it SHALL NOT.
- REQ-847 WHEN the saved file has a `pending` and its `tool_use_id` is a `tool_use` of the transcript with no `tool_result`, THE daemon SHALL set the permission `ask` as REQ-404 draws it, with `restored` true and the attention `permission`; IF the transcript has a result for it or no such `tool_use`, THEN the daemon SHALL drop the record and ask nothing.
- REQ-848 WHEN a permission without `agent_id` starts to wait, THE daemon SHALL save the conversation with its `pending` at once (REQ-392's save, forced), and WHEN it is answered or interrupted SHALL save it without `pending` before the answer is written to the engine.
- REQ-849 THE daemon SHALL NOT start an engine to restore an `ask`; the engine starts with the answer (REQ-754).
- REQ-850 WHEN a restored question has all its answers (REQ-749, REQ-750), THE daemon SHALL start the engine and write the user line of the table in Interfaces, one per question in order, the labels of a multiple choice joined by `, `.
- REQ-851 WHEN a restored plan is answered, THE daemon SHALL start the engine in the mode of the table and write its line; `no` SHALL leave the mode `plan`.
- REQ-852 WHEN a restored permission is answered, THE daemon SHALL start the engine in the conversation's mode and write the line of the table; WHEN the key is `yes` or `session` it SHALL also set the allowance for that tool and input (and the rule of `session`, as REQ-404 does); the tool of the ask SHALL end with the answer (`ok` for a question and an approved plan, `denied` for a refusal, `failed` with `lost` for a permission answered yes, whose call Claude makes again), because Claude's own settling of it is not on the stream; WHILE an engine runs already (a message sent meanwhile started it) the answer SHALL go to it and start no other, and `esc` or `^C` on a restored `ask` SHALL drop it with no engine to tell.
- REQ-853 WHEN a `can_use_tool` arrives with the tool and input of the allowance, THE daemon SHALL answer allow once and clear the allowance; IF it differs, THEN the daemon SHALL ask as always and the allowance SHALL stay until the turn's `result`.
- REQ-854 IF the engine of a restored answer cannot start (a held session, REQ-575; no `claude`), THEN the daemon SHALL refuse the answer with the error and keep the `ask`.
- REQ-855 WHILE an `ask` is `restored`, THE terminal client and the phone SHALL draw it as a live one with the line «↻ se retomó tras reiniciar · tu respuesta irá como un mensaje» above it (a row of the card on the phone), and no key or tap SHALL change.
- REQ-856 WHEN the daemon stops, THE daemon SHALL, after the save of REQ-638's order, end at once the process group of every engine that has an `ask` waiting, without closing its input or waiting, and SHALL close the others as REQ-638 says.
- REQ-857 WHEN an engine ends by itself with an `ask` waiting, THE daemon SHALL keep the `ask` as restored, with its attention, instead of clearing it, and the next answer SHALL start the engine as REQ-850 to REQ-852.
- REQ-858 WHEN the transcript has a `tool_use` with no `tool_result` in a turn that is over, or a `tool_result` with `toolDenialKind: "interrupted"`, THE daemon SHALL set the tool `failed` with `lost` true and the summary «sin resultado · pudo terminar»; IF the engine of the daemon ends by itself, THEN its running tools SHALL be `failed`, without `lost`, with «El motor se detuvo» as today.
- REQ-859 THE daemon SHALL NOT draw what Claude writes to settle an orphan: the `assistant` line of `model: "<synthetic>"` and the `tool_result` with `toolDenialKind: "interrupted"` of a tool whose answer the person gave.
- REQ-860 THE terminal client and the phone SHALL draw a tool with `lost` with `⏸`, dim, and its summary, not as a failure; a client that does not know `lost` SHALL draw it as `failed`.
- REQ-861 THE contract SHALL gain `Ask.restored` and the tool's `lost` as optional booleans, and no node's `v` SHALL change (REQ-7).

---
## Rationale
- RAT-1 **Fixtures, not generated code, are the contract.** Generated Kotlin from a schema is poor and hides drift; two hand-written sets of types checked against the same files cannot drift without a red test. The round trip (parse then write equals the file) catches a field one side drops.
- RAT-2 **A frozen envelope with versioned documents** (the user's point) means no forced daemon stop when a client or a document changes. The old jw bumped `PROTOCOL` 25 times, each refusing the old peer. A new daemon binary still needs a restart to run (it owns the PTYs), but nothing forces it for a JSON change.
- RAT-3 **JSON lines**, not length-prefixed frames with a binary kind: readable with `nc -U`, no framing code. Raw terminal bytes are not needed while a pane's screen is a node of text rows; add a stream only when a UI proves it needs one.
- RAT-4 **Looking is shared** (the user's call, replacing the first proposal of a per-client seen): if the phone cleared an aviso and the terminal kept showing it, the person would clear it twice. `mark_seen` carries the revision looked at, so an attention raised after the look stays. The cost is that a person who looks on one device no longer sees it as new on another.
- RAT-5 **RFC 6902 subset** is known, small, and easy to apply in Kotlin; array edits go by index and are safe because a patch always applies on exactly `from`.
- RAT-6 **One daemon is one session** in v1: a second session is a second daemon and socket (`JWN_SOCKET`). The session node's id is stable across restarts; its `epoch` is not.
- RAT-7 **The shell is the pane's process, and a program is started by it, not typed at it**: `$SHELL -i -c '<program>; exec $SHELL -i'` has no keystrokes to race the prompt or echo, still reads the rc files (`-i`), keeps the pane alive when the program ends (`exec` turns the same process into a shell), and does not depend on the shell's prompt. Each word is single-quoted for the shell that reads it (fish escapes inside the quotes, the POSIX shells close and reopen them). The id passed to a new agent is explicit, so two agents in one folder never share a transcript, and a restart resumes with `--resume`, never a reused `--session-id`.
- RAT-8 **Spans, not bytes.** The old jw kept each block's raw bytes and every phone ran its own terminal emulator (a Kotlin module with golden tests to keep it equal to the daemon's). Resolving colours once in the daemon (it already runs vt100) means clients draw runs of text and the terminal client, the phone and a future web client cannot disagree. The cost is a larger document per block, bounded by REQ-57.
- RAT-9 **Standard marks.** OSC 133 is what other terminals' shell integrations speak; the command text and folder use OSC 633 (VS Code's `E` and `P`) because 133 carries neither. The old jw used a private OSC 7770 and only for zsh; one set of marks now covers three shells.
- RAT-10 **A separate node, a field on the pane.** Blocks can be large and change on every line of output; keeping them out of the pane node keeps the pane's screen patches small and lets a client that only draws the screen never open them. The pane says where they are (`blocks`) so a client does not guess.
- RAT-11 **The editor is the client's, the line is the shell's.** The shell keeps its history, completion and key bindings; a client sends the finished line. Mirroring the shell's own buffer both ways (what the old jw did with zle's `$BUFFER`) is left for later (OPEN-6).
- RAT-12 **Two node types and four commands, not one reply.** A diff as the reply of a command would be read once and be stale; as a node it is opened like any other, patched when it changes (an agent's edits arrive on their own) and cached by clients with the same resume rules. The cost is that the node has to be made first: the command names the workspace and scope, and the daemon keeps one node per pair while someone uses it. The old jw answered a `diff` request and the phone asked again.
- RAT-13 **Lines as prefixed strings, no numbers.** `"+text"` is a third of the size of `{"k":"add","text":…,"old":…,"new":…}`, and the numbers follow from the hunk's starts, so a client that derives them cannot disagree with the daemon. Words changed and syntax colours are drawn by each client: they are a way of drawing, not data.
- RAT-14 **Marks belong to a file's content, not to a person.** `hash` covers the path, status and lines, so a file that changes after it was looked at says so (`changed`) without anyone comparing, and the mark is shared like attention (RAT-4): looking on the terminal clears it on the phone. The old jw kept them per phone.
- RAT-15 **Hooks for this run, and the daemon decides.** `--settings` is the one way to give an agent hooks without editing the person's own settings, and it merges with theirs; the file is read, not written, by claude. The hook command only forwards the event's name and two fields, so what an event means (and any later change to it) lives in one place, the daemon, and is tested with JSON alone. A prompt for permission and a plan are both permission requests, so the more specific one wins (REQ-134). The old jw passed hooks the same way but kept only `working`, `waiting` and `idle`, so it could not tell a question from a permission or say a plan was ready.
- RAT-16 (the sidebar says nothing of sessions since N47: its `^␣ s sessions` was a leftover with no key behind it) **One daemon is still one session** (RAT-6); the user decided on 2026-10-14, after asking whether several were needed. Worktrees already group by project and the finder (`^␣ ␣`) changes workspace fast; hiding the workspaces of another session would hide their attentions, which is what the product is for; and a session in the contract means more nodes and UI in two clients and a migration. What the old jw's sessions gave (a named list one daemon served, filtered by the client) is a client view, cheap to add later if a person keeps over about 30 workspaces or needs contexts that share one attention list (OPEN-10). Anyone who wants two separate contexts today runs a second daemon with its own `JWN_SOCKET`.
- RAT-17 **Looks are per client, closing is not.** A theme is taste of the screen it is shown on (the phone in daylight, the terminal in a dark room), so each client keeps its own and `settings.json` is read by the terminal client, not the daemon; the ten palettes are one file both read so they cannot drift (RAT-1). Each palette says whether it is dark or light (the user's call, 2026-10-14): a selector that offered a light theme in dark mode would be offering what the mode forbids, and with the kind written in the file neither client has to guess it from the colours. Closing a workspace or removing a worktree is left to the terminal (the user's call): a thumb on a phone is the wrong place for something that deletes a folder, and the phone only closes a pane. A worktree is never removed by force (the user's call): the old jw's `--force` threw uncommitted work away, so here a dirty worktree stays and the client offers to close the workspace only.

- RAT-18 **Pairing is a question for the person at the PC, so it comes to them and cannot be answered by accident.** `jwn pair` was a command in another terminal, which the person had to know to run; as part of the client it is `m`. The box opens by itself when a phone asks, since a question nobody sees is a pairing nobody can accept; but a key typed for something else (the `y` of closing a pane, a letter typed into a shell) must not accept it, so a question waits for the one already open, and `y` counts only after 0.8 seconds. Any other key refuses: the safe answer is the easy one.
- RAT-19 **The phone browses the PC's folders through the daemon, and only folders.** The terminal client reads the disk it runs beside; a phone is on another machine, so `list_dir` is one more command, and every other client that is not on the daemon's machine can use it. It lists folders only because the phone's job there is to open a workspace; reading a file is the viewer (N137 on, `read_file`, REQ-610). The terminal's browser and `list_dir` share one reading (`daemon::browse`), so they cannot disagree about what a folder holds. The phone first could not tell whether a folder was a repository, so it offered a worktree of any workspace and showed what git said (RISK-33); since N52 the daemon says it in `project` (REQ-222).
- RAT-20 **The programs are a file the person edits, read by the terminal client.** The user asked for it on 2026-10-15: a pane is always a shell, and `N` offers preconfigured programs (claude and the commands that start it) instead of a key per program. It is a file of its own, not part of `settings.json`: the client rewrites that whole file whenever a theme is chosen (REQ-152), which would erase what the person wrote by hand, and TOML takes comments and a `command` as a list, so there is no shell quoting to get wrong (the daemon quotes each word itself, REQ-50). Entries replace the built-in list instead of adding to it, so a person who does not use claude is not offered it. A file that does not read keeps the list the client had: a typo mid-edit (the client reads every 2 seconds) must not empty a menu that was working, and the reason is shown where the person will look. The phone is left out on purpose (its `+` is a shell, decided in N56); a list shared by both would be a daemon-side file and a new message, for later.
- RAT-21 **The line says what is asked of you, in colour, and the row is the button.** The user (2026-10-15) found the bordered «ir ›» pointless on a workspace's row and wanted the interaction seen in a colour that says it needs reviewing; a tinted strip and a row of its own were tried in the prototype and both rejected for «only the text», inside the same row under the detail. The colour follows what is asked and not the mark's tone: a permission's `!` is red and a plan's `◆` magenta, but both are a decision, and a decision is the one thing that must not look like the rest (yellow), a failure is red, and everything that only waits to be looked at is cyan (the colour of the merged flag; the prototype offers magenta and green and the user did not pick another). A tap target of its own is kept for the line (it opens the pane, not just the workspace) and it still gives the row's menu on a long press, so a person does not lose the menu by pressing on it.
- RAT-22 **The field draws the shell's line; it does not own it.** The user (2026-10-15) wanted the terminal's field like the phone's today, and chose that the shell stays the owner of the line (history, completion and its key bindings keep working: RAT-11) over a client-owned text like the phone's. To draw only what is typed, without the user's own prompt, the client needs to know where it starts, and the pane's screen alone cannot say: the prompt is the shell's. The daemon reads it where it already has the exact moment, the reader of the PTY, which processes the bytes in order and sees the prompt-end mark with the screen exactly at it; and it finds the first row of the line from the wraps of the screen, not from a row noted at that time, because a line that wraps at the bottom scrolls the screen and a noted row would be wrong in the commonest case. The text travels in the pane node, which already changes with every key, instead of a node of its own. The phone could draw it too (OPEN-6) and is not changed here.
- RAT-23 **The loading is decided by the client from what the daemon already sends.** A program that draws on the normal screen (claude) leaves the shell's screen in the pane until it paints, and since N62 the node is `full` from the moment the command is reported, so that old screen is exactly what the person saw for the first second (the user, 2026-10-15: «se ve la terminal original»; reproduced on a private daemon: 74 ms to 1.5 s). The user chose a loading over a blank screen. The running block's `out` is already the program's own output, resolved by the daemon, so «has painted» is «a row has more than blanks», with no new field and no new message; the client remembers per command that it painted (the program may clear its screen) and gives up after 20 s (a program that is quiet, like `ssh` waiting for a host, must not hide its screen for ever). The alternate-screen programs (nvim, htop) start on a blank screen of their own and have no loading. The phone's stop (REQ-262) is what its text promises.
- RAT-24 **A program that leaves its screen and takes a moment to end is «closing», for any program.** N64 hid the shell's old screen while a program starts; the same screen came back at the other end (the user, 2026-10-15, and «cualquier programa», with `nvim` and `mole` as examples): the real claude leaves the alternate screen at once and takes about 1.2 s to end (measured frame by frame in the terminal client: 158 ms after the second `^C` the pane showed the shell's prompt and `❯ claude`, until 1384 ms), and the node stays `full` until the shell says the command ended (REQ-251). The daemon already knows when a program enters and leaves the alternate screen; it says it in the one place a client reads each frame, the pane's `screen` (`alt`, additive, no version bump), and the client does the rest. The signal is the alternate screen and not a list of names, so it works for every program that uses it (claude, nvim, htop, lazygit, the screens of mole). The client counts the 3 s itself, from when it saw the program leave, so a program that leaves on purpose and keeps running (a shell inside nvim) is not hidden for good. The phone is not changed in this part (the user: the problem is in the terminal client); its `Screen` type carries `alt` and the same rule can be applied there.
- RAT-25 **The daemon is the terminal, so it answers the terminal's questions.** Measured, not assumed (2026-10-15, the user: «con nvim no muestra el de cerrar»): neovim, before it leaves, writes `CSI c` (primary device attributes) as a sync point and waits for the answer up to a second; the pane's terminal never answered, so every `:q` took 1.05 s with the screen frozen, and the same query ends the start of most TUI libraries (the real claude painted at 1.75 s without answers and at 1.15 s with them in a plain pty). Answering costs nothing, and it removes the wait instead of drawing it (N64, N65): `nvim` quits in 0.10 s (1.11 s before) and `claude` ends in 1.00 s (1.52 s) in a real daemon. What is answered has a fixed or a cheap answer. The colours (OSC 10, 11, 4) are not: they are the theme of the client that happens to be looking (a pane has none, or several), and a program that is told a background decides its palette from it, so a wrong answer changes what it draws; a program that asks colours together with the device attributes sees the attributes come back alone and concludes there is no colour query, which is what it concluded before. The cursor's place is read where the query stood in the output, not at the end of the read, because that is what the program asked about. The answers go through a channel to a thread that writes them under the writer's lock (REQ-268): the reader holding the lock of the input while a client's `write` is blocked on a full terminal input would stop draining the output of a program that is itself blocked writing it.
- RAT-26 **Drawing and reading cost what is shown and what changed, not what is held.** The user had to close `jwn` with the laptop slow (2026-10-15). A profile (`sample`) of a terminal client with a pane of 100 large blocks showed it at 100 % of a core and 412 MB while idle, in deserialising JSON and allocating, not in drawing: every turn of its loop (every 40 ms) cloned and parsed every document again, the blocks of the pane included, whether or not anything had changed (REQ-269); and each frame then built the lines of all the blocks to show thirty (REQ-272). The daemon, with a noisy command, spent its time in turning the running block's whole screen (up to 500 rows) into spans once per read (`block_of`), and it read each pane's screen and blocks once per event of a batch when the batch is there to be read once (REQ-270). The array diff compared lists position by position, so the oldest block dropping off a full list made every block differ from the one before it and the patch (and the 256 kept of them) hold the whole list (REQ-271). Measured on a release build, 160 commands of 600 lines each in one pane, idle: terminal client 100 % CPU and 412 MB before, 2 % and 67 MB after; a command that prints 1.5 million lines with a client attached took 116.7 s and the daemon 116.6 s of CPU before, 4.7 s and 3.8 s after. The limits (100 blocks, 4 MiB, 256 revisions) are the same: memory was bounded, not leaking.
- RAT-27 **«Sync» is a push, nothing else.** The user (2026-10-15) has no use for bringing a base's changes into a worktree: what the worktree has done is what has to be somewhere else. So `s` is `git push`, never forced, and what the remote has that the worktree has not stays for the person to bring with git in a pane, where a conflict can be seen. The push runs in a connection and a thread of its own because a network push may take as long as it likes and the terminal client has one thread: on the shared connection the spinner would stop and the screen would not answer. A push that has nothing ahead is not sent: it is answered from what git already knows, with no network.
- RAT-28 **The branch is what stays, the folder is what can be lost.** Removing a worktree takes its folder and never its branch (REQ-147), so commits that were not pushed are not at risk: the box says they stay. What is lost is what was not committed, and that is the one thing git refuses to take without `--force`. The user (2026-10-15) asked to be able to remove such a worktree after typing its name, as a repository is deleted on GitHub: the name is a speed bump against a key pressed by habit, and the daemon does nothing about it (the client sends `force` only after it). The phone sends no `force`: a worktree with changes is still refused there.
- RAT-29 **The layout is the daemon's, and is brought to agree instead of kept up to date.** The user (2026-10-15) wants the panels split, swapped and resized, and wants a project to open with a layout (`.jw.toml`, part 13): so it has to outlive the terminal client and be the same for every client, which is what the daemon keeps. Panes come and go in many places (a new one, a closed one, one whose shell would not start after a restart, a session saved before layouts), so one `reconcile` after every change gives each of them the right outcome (a closed pane's room goes to its neighbour) instead of every place having to remember it. A ratio is in thousandths so it is an integer in a JSON patch and equal on every machine. Directions are by where the boxes are on the screen, not by number: with splits the number says nothing about where a panel is. The phone has tabs and ignores the tree.
- RAT-30 **What the daemon saves is what a client would see.** The user (2026-10-15) wants a pane's history to survive the daemon, and the blocks are the history. They are saved as the blocks node has them (spans resolved, a running block as it is), a file for each pane so that `session.json` does not grow and a pane's history goes with the pane. A command that was running cannot be known to have ended, so it is kept as cut and says how long it ran as far as the daemon knew; a program that had the whole screen has no output to keep (`full`), and the shell's own screen starts afresh. The mark of a restart is a block, not a new node or a flag of the list, so a phone that does not know it shows an empty block instead of breaking, and ids stay unique. The time is told as «hace 2 h» and not as a clock time because no client here knows the person's time zone.
- RAT-31 **A press that may be a drag waits to be a click.** The terminal client used to act on the press (focus a panel, pick a block); with a drag possible it cannot know yet, so a press on text only records where it began and the click, if the pointer never moves, happens when the button is let go, at the cell it began on. A press on anything that is not selectable is the click at once, so nothing else changed. The selection is kept as lines counted from the first of the list and columns of the text, not as pixels or rows of the screen, so a scroll while it is held does not move it. The colour is chosen by contrast and not named (a fixed yellow would be invisible on a light theme, a fixed blue on a dark one) and the text on it is the ground or the text colour, whichever reads: «most visible for the theme» is the user's (2026-10-15). The copy is OSC 52, as the blocks' `c` and `o` already are, which Ghostty honours.
- RAT-32 **One folder at a time, and a search for the rest.** A tree of a repository with fifty thousand files cannot be sent whole nor kept in step, so the daemon answers one folder and the client asks again for each that is opened; what git says goes with the listing of the folder (one `git status` limited to it), so a folder shows its mark without the tree reading what is under it. Finding a file among thousands is a search by name, not a walk down the tree, and it is the daemon's (`git ls-files` already knows what is tracked and what is ignored). Both are asked in a thread and a connection of their own, as the push is, because git may be slow and the screen has one thread. A file opens in the editor of the person (`$VISUAL`, `$EDITOR`, nvim) in a panel, as the old jw opened it, and the Markdown viewer will take the `.md` the day it exists. The tree is on the right, drawn as the sidebar is, by the user (2026-10-15).
- RAT-33 **The phone lists files and does not open them (except a `.md`, N90, REQ-479), and does not leave the workspace.** The user (2026-10-15) asked that the phone navigate only inside the workspace's directory and open no files for now: so the phone needed no `read_file` (N137 adds it for text, REQ-610, RAT-63) and the PC's `list_files` already refuses a path outside the workspace (REQ-312). The viewer of text and of Markdown is its own part (the prototype's «vista / fuente» switch and its viewer are not built). A folder shows no count of what is in it, because the PC would have to list it to tell.
- RAT-34 **`.jw.toml` at the repository's root, read by the daemon, in the old jw's format.** The user (2026-10-15) chose the root of the repository, versioned, with three parts: where worktrees go and the branch's template, the setup with its output in a pane, and the initial layout with the default agent; ports and `.env` files are a part of their own. The old jw already writes this file in the user's repositories (`design-studio`, for one), so reading it unchanged means no repository needs touching, and ignoring what jwn does not use yet means a file may keep `ports` and `env` for the part that reads them. The daemon reads it, not the clients, because it is the PC's file and both clients (and the phone after) need one answer. **Setup is a pane of its own, not a step before the agent:** an agent's command line decides its hooks and its conversation (`launch::is_agent`), so wrapping it in a shell line that installs first would lose both; a pane has no exit to watch, and one that stays a shell keeps the output of a failed install where the person reads it. The cost is that the agent may start before the install ends. **`start_workspace` is a command of its own** and `create_worktree` stays git alone: a client that does not know it (the phone until N79) goes on with `new_pane`, and a failed layout leaves a worktree that exists. The branch's template has `{name}` only: when it is read there is no worktree and no base to name (the old jw's `{base}` was the default branch).
- RAT-35 **A pane is a shell and the agent is what runs in it, so the commands look at the foreground program.** The user said it (2026-10-15): the agent's panel is an ordinary shell panel with a `claude` or a `codex` inside. So there is no pane kind to ask for and no registry of agents: a layout's `run = "agent"`, `N` in the terminal client and a person typing `claude` at a prompt all make the same thing, and `read`, `prompt` and `pane ls` see it through `running.argv`. The commands use only what the daemon already serves because a command that needs a new message is a client that knows too much: they are the same `open` and `write` of every client. **A paste and then Enter, in two writes,** because an agent's input box takes a paste as text and treats an Enter that arrives with it as part of it; and **a quiet screen** as the sign that an agent is ready, because the hooks that exist (and only `claude` has them) say what an agent does, never that it started; `--now` is for an agent that is busy and takes the text into its queue. **`ls` reads the session only:** it is what a model asks first and what it asks often, and a workspace's panes are one `pane ls` away. The old jw's `pane open --run view:diff` and `view:md` are not here: those panes do not exist.
- RAT-36 **The engine is the daemon's, not the client's.** The user's picture (2026-10-15) is a terminal that is their workspace for many projects and a phone that is their workspace on the go, one hand, approving work «sin la necesidad del TUI». That only holds if the engine outlives every client: a turn started at the keyboard goes on with the lid closed, a permission waits for whoever answers first, and a monitor's line that opens a turn finds somebody to tell. So the process is the daemon's, as every pane's terminal already is, and the clients read one node.
- RAT-37 **A function of the interactive shell, not a program ahead of the original on `PATH`.** What is wanted is that `claude` *typed by the person* is jwn's, so a script, a hook or another tool that runs `claude -p` must get the original, and a function is exactly that: it exists only in the shell the person types in. `command claude` is the way out, for what jwn does not draw yet, and the binary runs the original itself for every argument the engine cannot serve (`-p`, `--version`, a subcommand), so a muscle-memory `claude --version` still works. It is a function of each shell the integration already starts (zsh, bash, fish: REQ-122), and an unsupported shell keeps the original.
- RAT-38 **The pane is still a shell (RAT-35); the conversation is how the clients draw what runs in it.** The user, the same day: «es el mismo shell pero cuando escribimos claude internamente lo cambiamos a nuestra versión de claude». So there is no kind of pane for an agent: the foreground program is `claude`, as it was, and what is new is that a client that understands the `conversation` node draws it instead of the terminal's screen, as it already draws blocks instead of the screen for a shell. The binary also prints a plain version into the pane's own terminal, so any other way of looking at the pane (a terminal that is not the client, `jwn read`) sees something.
- RAT-39 **The person answers, the daemon remembers.** A permission is the one place where the phone matters most (approving from a pocket), so the sheet and the box are the same question and the first answer wins. «Allow for this session» is a rule the daemon keeps (the tool, or a command's first words) and not the engine's own: the engine suggests rules (`permission_suggestions`, tested) but the form in which it takes one back (`updatedPermissions`) is not verified (RISK-50), and a rule that holds for every client of the conversation is the daemon's to hold. `bypassPermissions` and `dontAsk` are not offered, and `auto` is shown in yellow because it lets Claude act without asking (RISK-55).
- RAT-40 **jwn draws what the engine will not without its screen, and sends the rest as typed.** Tested, not assumed (2026-10-15): in this mode `/help`, `/status`, `/resume`, `/rewind`, `/tasks`, `/artifacts`, `/hooks`, `/permissions`, `/memory` and `/skills` answer «isn't available in this environment»; `!ls` is not shell mode; and `/cost` prints the subscription's use and not the conversation's. The user asked for every command of Claude's (the list of 115 on the author's machine plus what is only in the interactive screen), so the ones that only need data jwn has (the tasks, the artifacts, the skills, the list of conversations on disk) are screens of jwn's, and the ones that need Claude's own screen (`/login`, `/vim`, `/doctor`…) are not pretended: they say so and point at `command claude`. A command or skill is sent as it is typed because the engine expands it (the expansion is not in the stream, tested), so the client shows `/deploy` and never what it became.
- RAT-41 **The name and the colour are the conversation's.** Claude has `--name`, `/rename` and `/color` of its own; here the name and the accent live in the node, so the terminal and the phone show the same, and the name is given to the engine too so `claude --resume` in a plain terminal shows it. The colour is only an accent (the user, 2026-10-15) and one of the theme's six, never a free colour, so it reads in all ten themes (RAT-17).
- RAT-42 **The footer says three things.** The user (2026-10-15): mode and what runs in the background at the left, the model at the right, and the context is not shown. The cost of a turn is on the line that closes it, the limits and the effort are in `/status`, and `/context` and `/usage` answer the rest. A footer that shows less is one the eye can read at a glance when a permission is about to be answered.
- RAT-43 **Images are made smaller where they are taken, and `^V` is read by the client.** A photo of 3 MB is 3 MB on a phone's data plan and the model does not use more than about 1568 px on the long side, so the phone shrinks it (a JPEG, at most four of 5 MiB) before it leaves, and the terminal does the same before it sends; the daemon has no image library and does not transcode. A terminal only pastes text, so `^V` is a key the client receives and answers by reading the clipboard of the machine it runs on: it works beside the clipboard and not over ssh, where the clipboard is the server's (the client says there is no image).
- RAT-44 **The queue is the daemon's.** The engine documents queued messages but a write in the middle of a turn was not tested, and a queue kept by the client is lost with it; the daemon sends the next message when a turn ends, and an interrupt does not empty it (the user can see it, «1 en cola», and the messages are the ones he wrote).
- RAT-45 **The daemon parses Markdown and the clients draw blocks.** Two clients in two languages would need two parsers that disagree on lists, tables and what is inside a quote. One parse in the daemon (`pulldown-cmark`, the one the old jw used) gives both the same document, as the other nodes do, and a client's job stays drawing (wrapping to its width, its colours). The command reads only Markdown files of the workspace (REQ-466): the phone reaches it too, and «read any file» would be a way to read `.env` or a key. The plan's text is kept whole in the daemon (the engine gave it whole to ask for it), so the node's 200 lines of the box are not what the viewer shows. The cost is a reply of up to 1 MiB of JSON for a big file, once, when it is opened.
- RAT-48 **Editing is a change of keys, not a rewrite** (the user, 2026-10-16: «usa `toml_edit`»). A file the team versions, or one the person wrote by hand, has comments and an order that a file regenerated from the screen would lose; the screen changes the keys it shows and the daemon, which owns the file, leaves the rest alone (`toml_edit`, the one dependency this adds). The daemon compares each field with what the file says and writes only a different one, so a save of nothing is no change at all. A client saving into another file than the settings came from sends every field, because that file will say, and win with, what it holds: sending only the changes would make it forget the rest. Opening the file in an editor was left out (N97, Corrections).
- RAT-49 **The screen's colours are the blocks' spans, only on the alternate screen** (the user, 2026-10-16: «cuando cargamos nvim no veo que use el theme colors»; prototype `docs/prototypes/screen-colours.html`, approved with «solo colores y el teléfono después»). The pane's `screen` was text only (OPEN-2, closed with RAT-8's «styled output lives in `blocks`»), and the node `blocks` is in mode `full` precisely when a program draws its own screen, so nvim, htop and lazygit were the one place with no colour at all, not even their own. The daemon already turns a terminal's cells into `Span`s for the blocks (`rows_of`), so the screen takes the same type and the client the same drawing (`span_style`), and the ANSI colours 0 to 15 are the theme's the way they are in the blocks. `lines` stays so a client that does not read `spans` keeps working (an additive field, no new `v`, as `alt` was). `spans` go only with `alt`: a shell's screen is not drawn while its blocks are (REQ-65) and 25 workspaces of idle shells would each carry a second copy of their screen; a program on the normal screen (`command claude`) stays text for now. Measured on a real `nvim` with syntax colours and `termguicolors` in a 120×40 terminal: the screen is 9.3 KB with `spans` and 1.4 KB without; reading the screen takes 0.23 ms in release (1.8 ms before the cells were compared as the terminal has them and turned into colours only when a span starts). **What does not follow the theme:** a program with its own RGB colours (nvim with `termguicolors`, its default scheme) is drawn with them, and it picks them by the background it thinks it has; the daemon does not tell it (RAT-25: a pane has no theme and two clients may have two), so on a light theme nvim draws its dark variant. The user chose to build the colours only (OPEN-12). The phone draws the same rows (REQ-532) with the colours of its own theme.
- RAT-50 **Existing worktrees are a list to open from, not something adopted on its own** (the user, 2026-10-16: «si abro el proyecto jw en jwn no veo ninguno de los worktrees»; prototype `docs/prototypes/worktrees.html`, approved with «selector y teléfono después»). Jwn only knew the worktrees it made, so the ones git made by hand, the old jw or an agent never showed. The user chose a selector in the worktree box over bringing every worktree into the sidebar when a repository is opened: a repository can have tens of them, most of them dead, and a workspace for each would be a list to prune. The daemon reads `git worktree list` (it is git's own record, so a worktree anywhere on disk is found, in `<repo>-wt` or not) and a client picks. Opening makes only the workspace: the worktree is already there, so nothing is made, no file is copied from the checkout and the setup does not run (REQ-536; it would reinstall dependencies and rewrite `.env` files in a checkout the person already works in, and a worktree opened with `a` would start five setups at once). It has no panel for the same reason: opening five worktrees must not start five shells, and `claude` five times; the first panel comes when the person goes in (`↵`, `n`). The prototype said that going in starts the panel with the project's «al abrir»; the existing «este workspace no tiene paneles» screen does it with `n`, and changing what entering does for a workspace whose panels were closed is a separate decision. Ports are given as to any worktree, so two worktrees never share a block. The keys of the new side use the letters and `tab` (shell or `claude`), so the side is changed with `←` and `→`, which the name field does not use; the prototype's `tab` would have taken away the choice of REQ-510. The phone has the same two sides in its worktree sheet and a menu entry that opens it on the existing ones (the user, 2026-10-16: «selector y teléfono después»); it asks for the list the first time the side is shown too, and leaves the opened workspace with no panel, saying so. `changes` is read up to eight worktrees at a time, so a list of ten does not take ten times as long as one and a repository with fifty does not start fifty `git status` together. The list is asked for when the second side is first shown and not when the box opens, because the box blocks the client while the daemon answers and git reads every status: `w` to make a worktree stays as quick as it was, at the price of the count `existentes N` not being there until the side is looked at.
- RAT-51 **A resumed conversation is shown from the engine's transcript, read by the daemon** (the user, 2026-10-17: «al hacer un claude resume no trae y carga el chat en jwn», and «ni guarda los estados como el último modo o effort o modelo»). The engine does not say again what was said when it resumes, and jwn only had the items it saved itself (REQ-393), so a session made in Claude's own screen, in another pane or before N81 came back as an empty conversation with a divider, and in `manual` mode with the default model. The transcript is Claude's own record and the daemon reads it where `--continue` already looks for it (`projects_dir`, which also finds a folder named with `_` or a space made `-`, since it is not known which characters Claude replaces). The lines are turned into items by a reader of their own and not replayed through `apply`: `apply` is made for a live engine, with turns, queued messages and attentions raised, none of which a past conversation should do. Only the end is read, because the node keeps 300 items and a long session is tens of megabytes (RAT-52 says how far back); the reader begins at the next whole line. A tool with no result in the part read is shown as failed (the results are found by the tool's id, so a 4 MiB tail with 16,000 tools is read in 46 ms in release) (it was interrupted, or its result is out of the part): saying it succeeded would be false. The mode, the model and the effort are the ones the session ended with and are passed to the engine as flags, because a resumed engine starts from its own defaults: the transcript has `permission-mode` lines and, in each line of Claude, `message.model` and `effort`. What jwn saved for that pane is still preferred (REQ-393), as it has the commands and the colour. Tried with a stand-in engine and a transcript written like Claude's; not with the real `claude --resume` on a session of Claude's own screen.
- RAT-52 **The transcript is read, not copied; what is jwn's own is kept beside it by time** (the user, 2026-10-17: «la idea no es que jwn guarde una copia de esta transcripción sino que jwn lea la transcripción directa de claude», and «guardar la referencia» for the cost). A copy of the chat goes stale the moment another `claude` has the session open (the user ran this very session in both: the messages after the resume did not show), and doubles what is on disk. So the saved file keeps only what the transcript cannot say, and the time of each piece, and the chat is made from the transcript each time it is opened and while it is followed. The anchor is the **time**, not a `uuid` or a byte offset: the lines have a `timestamp` from when they were made, and a note, a cost or a divider made by jwn has a later time than the lines it follows, so «after the last line made no later than it» places it, where an offset would put a cost before the last line of its turn if the engine had not flushed it yet; the clock is the same machine's. The subagent's card is jwn's own (the engine's task messages are not in the transcript) but the transcript also has the tool that started it, so the card takes that tool's place and the chat does not show both. The transcript is read where it ends and as far back as it takes for 300 items, because a long session is mostly requests and attachments (this one is 380 MB for a few thousand messages: the real file is opened in 0.7 s, in release, with its 583 items and its mode, model and effort, and a look at it when nothing was added takes 0.2 ms); while it is followed only what was added is read, from the offset of the last whole line, so a line that is being written waits for its end. A conversation is followed with the node it was opened with: the answer of a look that took long is dropped if the pane has another conversation by then. A line of megabytes (a pasted picture) is read when it ends, and one of 64 MiB or more is skipped, so a long line cannot hold the follow for ever. Placing the lines takes only the end of them, so a session of tens of thousands of items costs the same as one of three hundred. The conversation stops being followed when its own engine starts, because the live items are then the truth and the engine writes the same transcript. **What is lost** when a conversation is made from the transcript again: who answered a permission (`by`) and the `live` state of a text, which the transcript does not have (the diff of an edit is not lost: it is made from the arguments of the tool, which the transcript has, REQ-556); the cost of each turn is kept (the transcript has the total, in a few `cost-state` lines, not each turn's). **What stays as it was:** a conversation saved before N101 (it has its own items and the transcript may not have been read) keeps them and is not followed, so nothing already saved is lost, and a conversation whose transcript is not there when it is saved keeps its items too (the saved file is not made lighter on a promise).
- RAT-53 **A resume with no session is a choice made before the conversation opens** (the user, 2026-10-17: «cargó el claude original no el UI que tenemos desarrollado… cuando hice `claude --resume`»). The engine is given a session to resume; `claude --resume` alone is Claude's own screen, with its list, and the daemon rightly refused it (`--resume needs a value`, REQ-373), so the binary ran the original and the person got Claude's screen instead of jwn's. The binary, which owns the pane's terminal, now makes the choice: the same conversations `/resume` lists (`saved_conversations`, read from Claude's folder in the folder `claude` was typed in, with no round trip to the daemon), in a list of its own drawn in the pane, and then opens the conversation as `claude --resume <id>`, which the daemon and the clients already serve (REQ-540 to REQ-546). The list is plain text in the pane and not the terminal client's: the client draws the conversation node, and there is none until a session is chosen; the person sees the list in the pane's screen and then the conversation. A search term (`claude --resume login`) filters the list as Claude's does; a value that is a session id is served as it is. A choice that cannot be made (the terminal cannot be raw) runs the original with what was typed.
- RAT-54 **What Claude Code shows between its tools is in the transcript, in lines the reader did not take** (the user, 2026-10-17, with two screenshots of the same session in Claude Code and in jwn: «si te fijas hay muchas diferencias»). Read against a real transcript: the narration between tools («Tienes razón, con `claude --resume`…», shown by Claude Code with «· summary») is a `thinking` block with its text, which the reader dropped as thinking; a message typed while a turn runs and absorbed in it is not a `user` line but an `attachment` `queued_command` (this is why the messages the user typed during a long turn were missing, 110 of them in this session); the end of a turn is a `system` line `turn_duration` (Claude Code's «Cogitated for 18s»). A `thinking` block with text is a summary here (155 to 247 characters in 9 blocks; 295 more were empty, redacted), but a model that gives its whole thinking would give pages, so only short ones are shown: a limit of 1,200 characters, a heuristic and not a flag, since the block does not say which it is. The items made from the lines are marked as read and not made by jwn, so a following that places the lines again does not keep them as jwn's own and show each twice. The lines that jwn's own engine writes of these kinds (20 commands, 3 interruptions, 10 system lines and an API error across 31 transcripts, all in files that Claude Code's own screen also wrote) are not made again, since the jwn that ran them kept an item for each and the chat would show them twice (REQ-557). What is left of the differences are of drawing and not of data: Claude Code folds consecutive tools into «Ran 2 shell commands» and shows the one that runs with its time, and has a working line with the time and the tokens of the turn; jwn lists every tool and has no working line for a session it only follows (RISK-61).
- RAT-55 **The tools are folded by the client from the items it has** (the user, 2026-10-17: «¿podemos agrupar los bash como hace Claude Code?»; prototype `docs/prototypes/conversation-fidelity.html`, approved). Claude Code shows «Ran 2 shell commands» and «Searched for 1 pattern, ran 3 shell commands» where jwn drew a row for each of the tools, which made a turn of thirty tools a page. Folding changes nothing in the node: the daemon says each tool, and each client groups the consecutive ones, so a conversation that jwn runs and a session it only follows fold the same, and nothing is stored. The classes are the ones whose output is not the point (search, read, list, shell); an edit is its diff, which is what the person wants to see, a subagent has its card, and a tool that runs or waits for the answer to a permission is drawn by itself because it is what the turn is doing. A run of one tool folds too, as Claude Code does, so the list does not change shape when the second tool arrives. Opening a run draws the tools exactly as they were drawn before (their own click still opens their output), so nothing that was reachable is lost; `^O` on a folded tool opens its run because a key that does nothing is worse than one more step. The words are written in `fold.rs` and in `Fold.kt` and no fixture shares them (the verb and the plural are the only things that can drift).
- RAT-56 **Markdown in a conversation is parsed by the daemon, once, when the text is finished** (the user, 2026-10-17: the tables did not render, «sí, hazlo con la opción A»; prototype `docs/prototypes/conversation-fidelity.html`). RAT-45 already settled that one parse in the daemon and clients that only draw is better than two parsers that disagree; here it is the same reader. It runs when the text is complete (a text that arrives is drawn as it comes and the blocks replace it), on the transcript's lines as they are read, and nowhere else: not on each patch, not in `follow`, not in `merge`. A text of plain paragraphs sends no blocks, so most items weigh what they did; and the client that does not know the field draws `text`. The reader recurses once for each level of quotes and lists, and a crash would take down the daemon, so a depth over 32 and a text over 64 KiB are not read as blocks (the viewer's reader gives a document that deep as one block of code). The blocks are not saved: the text is the source and they are made again when the file is read, so they do not halve the 2 MiB a conversation's file may hold.
- RAT-57 **The time of a tool is the item's own, and the tokens are counted by message** (prototype `docs/prototypes/conversation-fidelity.html`, approved). The daemon already stamps each item when it makes it, so `running` costs no field in the item (36 places build one) and no clock of its own, and the clients count the seconds as they count the turn's. The engine says a message more than once (a stream, then the line that completes it, one line for each block) and each time with its count, so a sum over lines counts it two or three times; the count is by `message.id`. The terminal's `kilo` and the phone's `convKilo` gave `1.2 k` and `1.3 k` for 1250 (one rounds a half to the even, the other up); both round up from the half now, and both write the decimal with a comma as `usd` does (`22,0 k`).
- RAT-58 **A turn of another `claude` is a fact of the transcript, and abandoned is a time.** The lines say when a turn begins (the person's message) and when it ends (`turn_duration`, or «[Request interrupted…]»), so the parser tracks it and the daemon does not guess from the process. A `claude` that crashed or was closed leaves a turn open for ever, and a tool or a subagent is silent for minutes, so no line for 15 minutes (the lines of a subagent count) is the rule; `follow_due` already runs each second and takes it up without reading the file again. It is a field of its own (`following`) and not `status`: a status of work would offer `■ esc`, an interruption and a queue for a conversation whose engine does not exist. The turns that jwn's engine wrote count too (`sdk-cli`), because a conversation of jwn that is followed after its engine ended must not look busy.
- RAT-59 **Older items are asked for by time, in a command, and the client keeps them** (the user, 2026-10-18: «sí, aprueba el prototipo con esas recomendaciones»; prototype `docs/prototypes/older-items.html`). `first` is not a place in the file: it is recomputed at each follow from the size of the window read and counts the items of jwn's own, so it cannot say where to read from. The time of the first item can: lines are written in time order, the daemon finds the line by halves, reads a chunk before it and a megabyte past it (so the result of the last tool of the page is there) and returns the items made before that time. In 1,217 item lines of the last 150 MB of the real transcript no two were in the same millisecond and none made two items, so the time names a place in the file; RISK-63 says what it costs when that is not so. The answer is a reply and not a bigger node because a node goes to every client: a page is 100 to 150 KB, and the phone would receive what the terminal asked for. Every answer goes back in time (a page with no items but more to read is still earlier than the last: its cursor is the time of the first line that was read), because a client that asks again with what it was given must come to the start.
- RAT-60 **The suggestion is the engine's, and jwn only draws it** (the user, 2026-10-18: «el suggestion en el text field, podemos traer eso a jwn»; prototype `docs/prototypes/suggestion.html`, approved). `claude` has `--prompt-suggestions`, which in print mode sends a `prompt_suggestion` after each turn; jwn does not call another model. Read in the binary (2.1.302), the engine does not send one in the first turns of a conversation, after an error or when what it would say is a thanks or a «let me…», and the call costs one more request for each turn: a conversation of one or two turns in a probe got none, so it was not seen arriving in a real session. Seen later (2026-10-18, `claude` 2.1.302, five turns with `--prompt-suggestions true`, the default permission mode, haiku): a suggestion after four of the five turns, `lee AGENTS.md ahora` and the like; and none in the `plan` mode, which the engine skips (`plan_mode`), as it skips a rate limit that is only a warning, a permission waiting and an error of the API. A conversation that jwn only follows, because another `claude` runs the session, has none by REQ-572. It is on by default, as in Claude Code, and `suggestions = false` in `config.toml` (the file the terminal client reads and `jwn claude` reads too) turns it off before the call. Enter with the field empty sends nothing so that a proposal is never sent without being read.
- RAT-61 **Another `claude` on the session is blocked, not warned, and it is looked for in Claude's own registry** (the user, 2026-10-18: «3 es mejor un bloqueo ya que claude code es el harness principal»; prototype `docs/prototypes/session-held.html`, approved). Two processes on one session each keep the conversation in their memory and append to one file, so the chain forks; the transcript cannot say that the other one is there, least of all when it only waits for a message and writes nothing. Claude Code writes `<claude folder>/sessions/<pid>.json` for each live process (session, folder, entrypoint, `busy` or `idle`, and the pid), which says it exactly. The check of the pid asks the system that it is alive and that its command says `claude` (a number may be reused), and leaves out only the engine that this conversation started last, which may still be ending when a restart begins; the engine of another conversation of the same daemon on the same session is another `claude`. A second jwn on the same session is another `claude` and is blocked as well. It cannot stop a `claude --resume` that someone opens later on a session that jwn already writes: that is the other process's to refuse.
- RAT-62 **The output of a shell comes from the file the engine writes, read by the daemon, not from the stream** (the user, 2026-10-20: «vamos con la 7»; prototype `docs/prototypes/shell-output.html`, approved). The stream of `stream-json` carries no output of a background shell: only its start, its end and a summary, and `output_file` only in the notification at the end (captures `crates/daemon/tests/captures/*.jsonl`, Claude 2.1.304: `task_started` has none, a Bash result names the file in its text and a Monitor result does not). Reading the file is what Claude Code itself does for `Read` on that path. A client never reads it: the phone has no access to the machine's files, and one place applies the rules of REQ-607.
- RAT-63 **A command of its own, not `read_markdown` widened.** Markdown comes back as blocks so that clients never get raw text (RAT-45); a text file is the opposite, raw lines, so it is its own answer and Markdown keeps its path. The jail is `read_markdown`'s (`files::inside`: canonical, inside the workspace), not `list_dir`'s, which takes an absolute path by design (REQ-202): a phone may ask this and no client may turn it into «read any file of the machine».
- RAT-64 **Cut instead of refuse over the cap.** The head of a log is useful and a refusal is not; the Markdown cap refuses because half a document cannot be parsed. Binary is refused with its own error code, not a message the phone would have to parse (`ErrorCode::Binary`; only `read_file` emits it, so an old client never sees it).
- RAT-65 **Lines as runs, like tool output's `Span`.** The text travels once, with no offsets: Kotlin strings are UTF-16 and Rust's are bytes, so offsets would drift on an emoji. A MiB of lines keeps the phone's memory small, and the 16 MiB line of the socket is far above any answer (the phone's inbound cap of 1 MiB is for what it sends, not what it receives).
- RAT-66 **`↵` views; there is no key that edits.** Reading is the common act and the spec already said «reading a file is the viewer» (RAT-19). N139 had `e` open `$VISUAL` in a new panel; N151 removed it because nvim does not render well in a panel (the user, 2026-10-20). A native editor is paused (RAT-77); until it is decided a file is edited in a shell panel with the program of the person's choice.
- RAT-67 **Colour in the daemon, in v1.** The user (2026-10-20) reads code with it, in every language. One tokenizer in Rust serves both screens, so the phone has none and the two cannot disagree; a client owns only the table from class to accent, which leaves every theme untouched. The rough tokenizer of `diff_lines.rs` guesses Rust-flavoured words for the terminal's diffs and cannot do TypeScript, strings across lines or the phone.
- RAT-68 **`syntect` with `two-face`'s grammars, pure Rust** (`two-face` 0.5.2, `default-features = false`, `syntect-fancy`; its default pulls a C regex). The user chose it (2026-10-20) over `tree-sitter`, which parses better but needs a crate and a C compile per language: «all of them» is one dependency here, about two hundred languages (the pack of the `bat` tool, TypeScript and Kotlin included). A language the pack lacks shows plain; adding one is adding a grammar.
- RAT-69 **The bounds of the colour: 64 KiB and 300 ms**, from a probe on this machine (release, no LTO): a warm parse runs at about 0.5 MB/s (200 KB of Rust took about 390 ms) and the first file of each language pays 225 to 480 ms to compile its regexes. 64 KiB is about 130 ms warm. The head is coloured and the rest is plain, never all or nothing: the colour is a nicety and must never be what makes a file slow.
- RAT-70 **The shell tells us its folder; the daemon does not read the process** (the user, 2026-10-20: «el shell tiene la referencia», no dependency needed). The integration already prints the folder before each command (`633;P;Cwd`); it prints it at each prompt too, so a `cd` shows as soon as the prompt returns. Reading it from the process needs `libc` and the first `unsafe` of the project on macOS, or an `lsof` every second.
- RAT-71 **`@` is written from the conversation's folder.** The engine resolves `@` against its own folder: with the engine in a subfolder, `@../top.txt`, an absolute path and `@in.txt` all became a `file` attachment with no tool call (captured 2026-10-20, 2.1.304, `--permission-mode auto`). So `../` is safe, and a path from the workspace was already wrong after `cd sub && claude`.
- RAT-72 **A conversation opened from a pane with no environment starts in the pane's folder, which is where the person is.** The engine finds a session by its folder, so `/resume` lists the conversations of where the panel is; `jwn claude` keeps sending the shell's `PWD`, which is the same folder.
- RAT-73 **`--chrome` at the engine's start, on by default** (the user, 2026-10-20: «yo uso bastante el tool de chrome»). Tested with the real engine (2.1.304, headless stream-json): with `--chrome` the server `claude-in-chrome` is `connected` and its tools answer; without it there is none, and that is how jwn started the engine. `/chrome` sent as a message is answered «isn't available in this environment» and adds nothing, so the flag is the only way. The tools arrive deferred, so on by default costs their names only.
- RAT-74 **The tree is asked for before it is wanted, and an old answer is shown while the new comes** (the user, 2026-10-20: «¿por qué el filetree no está precargado?»). After N147 the daemon answers a folder in 0.05 to 0.1 s, so what was left was the client waiting to be asked: the terminal asked at `t` and the phone at the files screen, which blanked at each folder. A listing is small and the daemon's read is cheap, so it is asked as the workspace is selected; a stale list is shown with the old rows until the answer replaces them (stale-while-revalidate) because a file tree that changed in the last minutes is better than an empty one.
- RAT-75 **A double click is a drag over the word, made by the client** (the user, 2026-10-20: «doble click en una palabra para seleccionarla»). Every list already had a drag with a selection and a copy on release, so a second press on the cell widens that drag to the word and the release does the rest: one way of copying on three surfaces. The terminal has no double-click event, so the client counts two presses on a cell. The shell's own field (the line the shell holds) is not selected: the shell owns it. The word takes `.` `/` `-` `~` so that a path or an option is one word, and the letters are `char::is_alphanumeric`, so `ñ` and `ö` are letters. Cmd+click on a URL (the user's other request) is not here: whether the terminal passes it on is measured first with `mouse_probe.py`.
- RAT-76 **A daemon that stops ends its engines; it does not hand them to the next one** (the user, 2026-10-20: «cuando jwn se cierra claude se queda en otro pid… ¿no podemos hacer un reattach?»). `Engine::stop` closed the engine's input and ended its group three seconds later from a thread, and a daemon that exits takes that thread with it: an engine in the middle of a turn, or with a background task, ignores the end of its input and was left running with no daemon, holding its session, so the next daemon's `--resume` was refused by REQ-575 («otro claude tiene esta sesión abierta»). The fix waits for the engines before the process exits and notes their groups for a daemon that is killed. Reattaching to the same process is not possible as the engine is built: its stdin and stdout are pipes that belong to the daemon, and they end with it. See OPEN-21.
- RAT-77 **No editor in jwn for now** (the user, 2026-10-20: «vamos a quitar e de que abra nvim… no renderiza bien»). A native editor with Neovim as the engine over `nvim --embed` was spiked (works: the user's LazyVim loads, `lua_ls` attaches and sends diagnostics, the visual range comes from nvim, 4 ms from a key to a frame; `~/.local/share/jwn-tools/nvim_embed_spike.py`) and is paused; the phone would never render it. The viewer gets a cursor, a selection that hands Claude a reference, and tabs instead (N152, prototype `docs/prototypes/viewer-pick-lines.html`).
- RAT-78 **The viewer is where a person reads and asks about code, so it has a cursor and tabs** (the user, 2026-10-20: «hjkl para moverse, v abre la selección y poder usar tabs»). The cursor is only for choosing lines to hand to Claude, so it has `h j k l`, `e`, `b`, `v`, `↵` and the keys the viewer already had, and not the rest of Neovim's motions; where it collides (`j` `k` `h` `l`, which scrolled, and `b`, which was a page up) the cursor wins, and `y`, `w` stay as they were. The reference is the diff's (REQ-592) and goes through the same field, from the folder the agent runs in (REQ-631), with the path the file was asked by and not the canonical one (a workspace under a symlink, such as `/tmp` on macOS, would not strip). `/memory` rows used the editor; they open the viewer when the daemon can read them. Not built: the `●` mark on a tab whose file changed on disk (the daemon does not tell), and `V` of the prototype's Neovim-like first version.
- RAT-79 **The phone does not scroll a file sideways, and picks lines by their numbers** (the user, 2026-10-20: «no quiero que haga overflow hacia la derecha… ni el visor ni el diff»). A phone screen holds 40 to 50 characters of code, so a long line cut and scrolled hid more than it showed, and a toggle for wrapping was a decision the person had to make for every file; the lines wrap always. Long press already selects text the system's way, which gives no line numbers, so a tap on the number picks lines: the number is a column apart from the text and does not take part in a selection. The row of a number is 24 dp high and 48 dp wide, lower than a touch target of 48 dp because a line is 16 sp: the bar that acts on the picked lines has the 48 dp targets. The draft of lines travels in the client's state (`ClientState.draft`) and the workspace's screen hands it to the pane that runs a `claude`, because the files screen does not know the panes (it only has the workspace). The same for what scrolled by accident and with no word of it in the spec: the diff and the output of a tool inside a conversation, and the code blocks of a Markdown (REQ-644). A table keeps its columns, because wrapping every cell of a wide one would make a column a word wide, and a terminal's screen keeps its own: they are laid out by their width.
- RAT-80 **Jobs live in «avisos», apart from the alerts** (the user, 2026-10-22, after seeing the prototypes): an alert is something that waits for the person, a job is something that happens or happened; mixing them made `avisos N` mean two things, and a failed removal is not a question. So one screen with two tabs, the bell and `avisos N` count alerts only, and the jobs have a small mark of their own. A failure keeps its cause in the `trabajos` tab and a `✗` on the row, and leaves no alert.
- RAT-81 **The list of jobs is a field of the session, not a node of its own.** Every client has the session open already and receives its patches; a node of its own would need a type in each client's `hello`, a place to find its id and a second subscription, for a list of at most twenty small items that changes only when a job begins, ends or is dismissed. The time a job has been running is not in the document (the clients count from `started_at`), so nothing patches while it runs. Only `remove_workspace` with `remove_worktree` answers `{job}` and runs behind the reply: the push (`sync_workspace`) stays as it was (REQ-652) because its clients already wait for it in a worker and cancel it, and turning its reply into a job would change what REQ-276 and REQ-604 say for no gain.
- RAT-82 **A removal is not cancelled**: stopping `git worktree remove` half way leaves a folder half taken and a worktree git still lists; a push that is stopped leaves nothing. So `cancellable` is true only for the push.
- RAT-83 **Why option-backspace erased a character in the composer and the shells, measured on the user's Ghostty (2026-10-22, `macos-option-as-alt = false`).** Without keyboard flags the terminal sends `esc DEL` for option-backspace and `esc BS` for option+ctrl-backspace; with the flag `disambiguate` on (1, which the client asks for so that shift-enter is told from enter: `CSI 13;2u`) it sends a plain `DEL` for option-backspace (the option is dropped: it composes) and `CSI 127;7u` for option+ctrl-backspace, and `CSI 127;5u` for ctrl-backspace. A plain `DEL` is a backspace: no client can tell them apart, which is why option+ctrl worked and option alone did not. With `report all keys as escape codes` (flags 8, 9, 13, 31) the terminal sends `CSI 57443;3u CSI 127;3u` (the press of the alt key, then backspace with alt) and so reports the alt, but it changes how every typed key arrives (a composed `ñ` or an accent among them) and adds an event for each modifier key; that was not tried. The fields that were a `pop()` read no modifier at all, which was a second, separate cause (REQ-673); `esc BS` and `CSI 8;…u` had no arm (REQ-670). crossterm's decoding of each encoding was checked in a pty (`esc DEL` and `CSI 127;3u` are backspace+alt, `CSI 127;7u` backspace+alt+ctrl, `esc BS` is `h`+ctrl+alt, `DEL` backspace).
- RAT-84 **↑ and ↓ follow the rows that are seen** (the user, N162): a line that the field wraps shows as several rows, and an arrow that jumps the whole line breaks what the eye sees. The editor learns the width of the field from the last frame (the width its text wrapped to) and counts the rows as `field::wrap` does; before the first frame, with no width, a row is a line. A test makes both agree.
- RAT-85 **A group is a level inside the one session, owned by the repository** (the user, 2026-10-22: groups of workspaces in one session «para consumir menos del equipo», toward an «agentic software factory»). RAT-16 stands: several sessions would mean a daemon each, and hiding the workspaces of another would hide their attentions. A group is organization only, so it can be deleted without closing anything. **One group each**: the sidebar is a tree with one cursor, and a row that appears twice counts its attention twice. **By repository**, under `project` (REQ-685): moving a root moves its worktrees, a worktree made later is born in the group, and closing and opening again brings it back, without a key of its own for each workspace (a workspace's id would be new at each opening). The name is «grupo»: `project` is the repository's `.jw.toml`, «espacio» reads as workspace and «producto» is too narrow for a client or a set of ideas; `grouping.rs` keeps its name and, in the spec, orders «by repository» (REQ-219) while «group» is only this. Groups are the person's, not the repository's: they are not in `.jw.toml`, which travels with the code. The binary gets the same commands so that an agent can sort the work (the brain is a pattern, not a feature of jw). The view of a group as a table of its agents (the «fábrica» of the prototype) is not in this plan (OPEN-38).
- RAT-86 **Folding is each client's** (the prototype's option, chosen on 2026-10-22 as the default): the terminal in front of a person must not fold because the phone did, and a client forgets nothing it needs (a group it does not know any more is dropped). It is also one field less in the contract and a change that reaches no one else. The phone keeps it apart from `Seen`, which is reset when the daemon's epoch changes. The keys `g`, `G`, `h`, `l`, `z`, `J`, `K` are free at the workspace level (RISK-80), and `J` `K` already mean «move down, up» to swap panes; the digits stay what they are, a label: no key jumps by number (OPEN-39).
- RAT-87 **The window has a place, the limit follows the pane** (the user approved the prototype `docs/prototypes/composer-scroll.html` on 2026-10-22 and took its recommendations as the defaults): today `wrap` keeps `start = min(rows − 5, cursor row)` and works it out again on every frame, so moving down from the top of a long text keeps the cursor glued to the first row while the text slides under it, and nothing says that there is more text out of sight. A window that keeps an offset and moves it the least that shows the cursor lets the cursor move inside it. `Field` is made again on every frame and cannot hold the offset; the `Ui` holds it, in a `Cell` as it holds `text_width`, because `draw` takes `&self`, and only the draw stores it (REQ-704). The limit is a third of the pane's height (at least five) so that a taller terminal gives the person more of what they write; the list keeps four rows. The prototype counted `ih − 1 − 4 − 4` as the room; the real layout also has the line of work and its blank row while a turn runs, so the spec counts them always (REQ-703) and the band does not jump when a turn starts. The phone's composer is Compose and scrolls by itself: out of this part.
- RAT-88 **The composer is `Composer`, and the shell's field is the same field** (the user, 2026-10-22: «deberían de ser el mismo»): the type that holds the text is `Editor` (`conversation.rs`) and the file that draws it, `field.rs`, draws also the field of a shell pane, so the type is renamed `Composer` in a commit of its own that changes no behaviour (N165a) and the file keeps its name. REQ-254 to REQ-258 are the shell pane's requirements and the conversation's composer inherited them by sharing `Field`. N165a to N165d gave the limit, window, bar and wheel to the composer alone, because the text of a shell pane is the shell's, drawn from `input`, and its offset needs a place in the client for each pane; two fields that look alike and behave differently surprised the person, so N165e gives the shell the same (REQ-708), with its offset in the `View`.
- RAT-89 **A fold is one private-use character with a table beside it, not a list of atoms** (the prototype's atoms are a `{c}` or a `{t, n, body}`): the composer is a `Vec<char>` and the cursor, the selection, Backspace, Delete, `word_back`, `mention` and `slash` are written over chars. A character of `U+E000 ..` for each fold (and a table from it to the pasted text and its number) makes all of them take a fold as one step without being touched, and puts the work in two places: the width of a character in `field.rs` (a fold is its label's) and `text()`, which gives the real text. The price is that the block must be taken from what is typed (REQ-712, RISK-87). `[Imagen#n]` is plain characters today (`attach`, `conversation.rs`), not an atom as the request thought, and is left as it is (OPEN-44).
- RAT-90 **The daemon writes the history, by repository** (the request left it to the spec): every client's send goes through `conversation_send` and the daemon already makes the repository's key (`project.rs`, the same for a worktree and its main folder), writes private files whole and 0600 (`history.rs`, `mobile.rs`) and is one writer when two terminals are open on one repository; a file the client wrote would race and would never be the phone's. It costs a contract change (`folds`, `conversation_history`, N166d). The path is in the state directory and not the config's (`~/.config` is often in a dotfiles repository, and `projects/<key>.toml` is there); `sent` and not `history`, which holds the panes' (`<state>/history/<pane>.json`).
- RAT-91 **↑ recalls and the list keeps the wheel and the page keys** (the user's prototype, 2026-10-22, taken as the default; they said «perfecto» to the list without answering each point): it is what the person expects from the old pane that ran Claude Code. Page keys and the wheel exist and scroll the list from the field (REQ-411, REQ-707, `conversation.rs`); what is lost is ↑ on the first row as a one-line scroll, which stays when there is nothing to recall. The alternative, `ctrl+p` and `ctrl+n`, is free and changes nothing that exists.
- RAT-92 **Folds at five rows or 300 characters, the beginning in the label, numbered by message, the list folded at five rows** (prototype defaults): the label tells what was pasted and the beginning tells which paste it is.
